**The March's tin is *day minus nineteen* and the rule is unchanged and unbroken**: day 85 = 66 · 88 = 69 · 91 = 72 · 94 = 75 · 97 = 78 · 100 = 81 · **104 = 85**, and it stands at eighty-five days at risk on the last day of the batch, unmoved, on a bed at three and a half degrees. The dry-land line is **no longer recorded as lost**, and § *Proposed canon changes* below carries that change and the reasoning.

## Numbers and records established in Chapters 69–76

A later writer must not change these without recording the change. Every figure below is additive. **No figure established in Volume 01 or in Volume 02 Batches 0001 and 0002 was altered.**

| Fact | Value | Established |
|---|---|---|
| **The collar bought from stock** | **two shillings, sold and not made.** Coll Renner sent it up the fen road on the twenty-fifth in a fist-sized parcel with a foreman's note and a second note in his own: *I did not make this. I am selling one I cut in the spring of this year and I have four left and I am not selling the four. If you want one to go up that road faster than a Tuesday cart, that is money, and I will say no, and I would rather say no in writing on a Sunday than in a yard on a Wednesday.* Both notes were copied into the book word for word | Ch 69 |
| **The seed house's tin** | **eleven shillings before the collar, nine after**, and the nine were the seed house's float for a month in which a bag of lamp oil was not going to be bought. Lissa Vale said so in one sentence and did not soften it and did not ask anybody, and the amount was read aloud before it was entered. **Four shillings from Ottoline Fray went in on the seventh, making thirteen**, and the man of about fifty read the amount aloud because the rule of the twentieth of last month says a person reads an entry before it is initialled | Ch 69, Ch 74 |
| **The fen's own eleven years** | **ninety-one lines in eleven years, of which forty-one are the lines the index wanted and four had never been indexed by anybody including the steward who kept them.** Two hundred pages when set. A shilling a copy. Printed and sold to anybody in the yard, on a Monday, for as long as there is anybody left to sell them to | Ch 70, Ch 73, Ch 76 |
| **The certificate of origin** | **a sheet of printed paper on the letterhead of Fray and Daughters, three ruled lines with printed heads and no others: *ground*, *season*, *keeper of record*.** Two hundred went out with the buyer's winter list in the spring and **thirty-one had been used.** Four shillings a lot. The lot itself eleven pounds. A wetted lot of thirty-one to forty grains laid against a bead reading in four seconds | Ch 70 |
| **The device cannot name a person, and the seller fills the third line** | **the third of the three lines, *keeper of record*, is in ink and is the name of a man whose shed the seed was not grown in, and the woman who sells the lot fills it in, and the ground and the season on the same sheet are true and the keeper is chosen.** Four hundred lots read and never once a name. **This is the volume's discovery made visible on a form, and it is Tova Reed's line, not the buyer's** | Ch 70 |
| **Eleven parts carrier, one part line** | **the Marrowfield lot is nine parts of a Marrowfield carrier and one part of the March's dry line, grown out from a cutting, and the certificate does not say so and there is no column for it and the sheet would be a different shape if there were.** A lot of one part in eleven is a legal sale and the trade has agreed to call it one. The first season's harvest is the line; the seed saved off it and sown again is the carrier, and nothing in the trade will tell a man which he has got | Ch 70, Ch 74 |
| **Ottoline Fray** | **forty-one, of Fray and Daughters at Marrowfield, four generations, a counting house, forty-one smallholders, twenty-two years in the trade, and she is the third of her name and says so at the gate before she is asked.** She gave her name and her house at the gate and Dunmar Fitch entered both with the hour and read them back to her. She has a collar, bought in the spring from a man she names, and the price was not entered. She asked for nothing and offered two dates | Ch 70 |
| **The offer, and the terms** | **a shilling a line on ninety-one lines, two pounds a month, paid at the quarter, term eleven years, this holding named as the keeper of record on every certificate and forbidden afterward to say the seed was certified by anybody else.** Her own statement of why the term is eleven years: *at the end of it I hold your history and you hold a promise and the promise is the cheaper of the two things* | Ch 70 |
| **Tova Reed's arithmetic, and the answer** | **two pounds a month is eight shillings, compost is four shillings a hundredweight, so the offer buys two hundredweight of the three hundred and ninety-nine hundredweight short, which is five hundredths of the gap, which is nothing.** And: *it is not a price, it is a lease on the only thing in this fen that cannot be grown.* The number of copies she thought might sell in a year was penciled underneath the eleven shillings and **struck out, with the clerk initialling the striking**, the second time in a season she has made him initial a striking | Ch 73 |
| **The first reading, and what the device can and cannot do** | **the fen's own saved bean read as this fen, the low strip above the toe ditch, after a ley — correct, exact, and the yard's discovery is that the device cannot tell a field from a holding.** The March's wet-line rye from the flowerpot read as the March, the wet ground above the second sluice, last sowing, a ley the season before, and it is a control. **The grafted seedling from the same pot did not read a place at all** | Ch 69 |
| **What a grafted lot reads** | **four seconds, and not a ground and not a season but an order: the thirtieth of last month, the fifth hour, the north annex, a cut out of a pot and a cut out of the field bed, a tie of green withy about nine inches, and a decision to keep one of two and throw the other away.** Lissa Vale identified it as the ninth tray of the sowing of the second year back, the one with a black bend in the stem, kept against the drill because it was the worst of the forty-one and the only one with a clean root. **The device reports the last hand** | Ch 69 |
| **Lissa Vale's refusal, and the first hole in the book** | **she refused the reading, in nine lines, in her own words, and the clerk entered the refusal with her name on it and not the thing's.** What went in the book was the fact of the test, the hour, the number of lots, and the two results that were places. Her reason, the second time in a season: *there is nothing in it that a man with a rule can check, and a line in a book that cannot be checked is a line somebody can use, and I have watched a woman in a market be indexed twice and I am not going to be the reason there is a third time.* **This is the first thing this holding has declined to enter since the book was opened and the fifth sheet was adopted, and the rule of the twentieth of last month survives it** | Ch 69 |
| **The floor, and the class of thing** | **thirty-one trays and nine tins came down off three shelves at the ninth hour of the third of next month, the lamp on the floor, the fieldbook on a chair, and Lissa Vale read out what was in each thing as she set it down in an order nobody else could reproduce. Of the thirty-one, six read as a place and those six were the six that had come out of a bulk sack or a tin and had not been chosen by anybody since; the other twenty-five read as a hand, a season, and a decision.** Anneke Roos had the class named on the page before the word *seedkeeper* was written over it: *a lot that has never been chosen by anybody reports where it was grown, and a lot that has been chosen by anybody reports who chose it and what they chose, and the second one is a better answer and it is not a better fact* | Ch 72 |
| **The cutting list** | **eleven names, in a clerk's hand, under a heading in Tova Reed's own hand, dated the eleventh month two seasons back, the year the dry line failed.** The eleventh name is this fen. There is no column for cuttings in the trust's book, because cuttings are not seed, and it is in no book at all, and she has carried the sheet in her case for two seasons. Her stated reason, given out loud in the room: *it was the only thing I have ever done that I could not put in a column, and I did it because the line was dying.* **She says she would do it again and asks the room to write that down, and nobody writes it down, and Lissa Vale declines to read it back, and that refusal is the answer** | Ch 72 |
| **The ninth line against the trust's interest** | **entered on the fourth of next month at the ninth hour, in daylight, at the middle of the table, in her own hand, in the trust's book as well, and it goes on the Thursday post to Cray.** It says the dry-land line of the trust **was not lost**, was cut and dispersed to eleven holdings, and that three books have carried the statement that it is lost, and that the statement is hers and is a wrong, and that she cannot name a ground of the eleven that she has seen this season, and that the proof is a certificate of origin on a letterhead and a collar and a buyer, **and that the proof was correct when it was given, and that she will not have it softened by anybody including her.** It is the ninth line and it is the largest of the nine | Ch 72, Ch 74 |
| **The four lines never indexed** | **entered in the fen's book on the fourth, read out in the yard on the seventh, and going into the printing. The fourth of the four is the Ashcombe oat, and Lissa Vale read it aloud as the fen's own keeper.** Entered: the lot entered under the printed name of the Ashcombe black oat eleven years ago, by her, at the counting house at Cray, in the eleventh month, out of a tin that had come north in a cart with no label on it, through a man who wanted to be helpful, not able to be established, and an unestablished name is not sown, and this holding had nothing else that season and thirty-one households would have gone into the winter without a bean, and she wrote the name down. **Tova Reed's two conditions for saying it in a yard: that it was a decision about food and not about a name and that she would take it again, and that after the seventh her name is on every copy of the printing in this district** | Ch 74 |
| **The vote of the seventh** | **taken standing, in daylight, in the yard, with the book shut under the clerk's hands. The buyer from Marrowfield was at the gate for the whole of it, on the clerk's ruling, because a body that takes a decision a buyer has paid for in a yard has already lost it. Seven hands could be counted and seven were in the yard. Five for the printing and against the certificate. Two against, and one of the two was the engineer of record, who put his own name in the book against his own vote and said that a man who says a thing twice has stopped being a witness and become an advocate.** Each hand named and entered as it went up. The woman with the shilling voted for the printing and said why in one sentence, which is her standing in that yard | Ch 74 |
| **Lissa Vale's vote** | **she put her hand up first and would not say which way, and refused to state it, and gave the reason: the last thing a reader does is tell a room what a page means before the room has read it. The clerk entered that she declined, and the reason, and that she did not decline to vote. Nobody thanked her and nobody said a word to her about the ninth tray or the forty-one trays, and there is no clause in a charter or a fifth sheet or a book about a woman being thanked** | Ch 74 |
| **The trust's money** | **eleven shillings out of the trust's printing account at Cray at a shilling a page without reference, which is the account and not Tova Reed's discretion, on a standing order honored six years running. If the clerk in Cray disapproves, the eleven shillings come out of her own hand and not out of this holding's box, and that is entered, and the clerk is a man named in a letter and is not named in the yard** | Ch 74 |
| **The full-draw load test** | **performed on the first of next month, a Saturday, on the district's order of the twenty-fourth of last month over Hilary Skeath's name in ink, with Coll Renner present as the licensed engineer, the paper the district's, the holding not to have a copy, and Marek's own undertaking of the twenty-fourth of last month that he would not have the first copy, which the district did not want. Pump to eleven hundred at the ninth hour and held one hour and ten minutes, and **the ten extra minutes entered in the fen's own book before the figure, because a test longer than the order is a different test.** The head held eleven hundred and the frame hummed from about ten hundred and forty and the Longneck came up eleven degrees and the pit was not up a quarter of an inch. The bed was not fed for the hour and ten, entered as a fact about an hour | Ch 71 |
| **The leak, under the load, for the first time** | **five-eighths of an inch at the ninth hour and forty minutes with the pump at eleven hundred, twice measured, the second rule reading smaller — the same figure it has stood at for thirteen days, and the same figure the leak has carried every day since the first of a month two months back.** It did not move under a load nobody in this district has ever applied to it. **It is not mended, it is not diagnosed, it is a reading and not a finding, and nothing was written underneath it about why.** It goes into the housing and not into the pit. Kellan Rusk's spring statement that it cannot be mended under load stands and is not superseded | Ch 71 |
| **The eleventh logging of the beat** | **the first, the second and the ninth in the quarter hour with one run of four, at the rail and not at the coping stone, cause not established, licensed person present ONE, which it had never been.** Nothing was written underneath it about what a licensed man eight feet away means, and it is entered as a contact and not as a finding. **It is not diagnosed and it is not going to be** | Ch 71 |
| **The third of the three conditions in clause three** | **put on the table at the examination and still not described, and the answer is the whole of the chapter's hinge: *the third condition is tested at an examination, and no condition is tested twice in the same term, and the term ends with the charter*, and the examination at which it is tested is therefore the first examination held after the term ends** — which is the first examination held after the twenty-ninth of the month after next, at which **the holder then is the holder then** and will be told in a room with a district officer in it, on a form, with no season's notice. Gael Senn wrote the form's words and then wrote her own second line and read it back and the clerk did not stop her. The clerk asked to go home and was not refused, and that is entered | Ch 71 |
| **The test and the eighty a day** | **eleven hundred at the head, seven hundred and twenty taken by the ground, three hundred drawn at Kellan's pump house, eighty a day unaccounted for for six years, one hundred and seventy-five thousand two hundred, and the test does not account for a gallon of it.** Marek's entry in his own words: a test at the head measures the head, a number about a mouth and not a number about a place, the same thing as the panel of the twelfth of last month in a different instrument, and the panel is in no book | Ch 71 |
| **The printing** | **two hundred pages, set at Fennmere in a week, eleven shillings, forty copies, the paper already in the shop. Off the press on the fourteenth and carried up the fen road on a hand barrow.** Last page: Coll Renner's two hundred words on what a bead reads and does not read and what one part in eleven means; under them Tova Reed's two sentences, one of which is that a lot of one part in eleven is a sale the law allows and the trade has agreed to and that no office in this district will call it fraud, and that is a notice and not an opinion; under those, his name and his license number. Lissa Vale read all two hundred pages out in the yard on the fourteenth. One copy sold in the yard at a shilling, one to the buyer's house | Ch 74, Ch 75, Ch 76 |
| **Coll Renner's offer, and the refusal** | **twelve pounds — his quarter — for a collar of a stated size and a stated bead, and a standing instruction that collars be cut on a Sunday, when no holding is holding an examination and no licensed man is on a road. Two men, not one, and he has said two because there were two. He refused in writing on the ninth, kept a copy, dated and signed, and sent the letter back by the hand that brought it, and came up the fen road on the tenth, five days before the fifteenth he had promised in a yard on the eighth, and gave the fen the offer before he said anything else** | Ch 75 |
| **Why he refused, and the fen's own rule** | **a man who makes a bead on a Sunday is a man whose beads have no record, and a bead with no record is worth whatever the man selling it says it is worth** — and the rule he quotes back at them is the fen's own, made in the spring, that no work on this node is done between dusk and dawn without a licensed engineer standing in it, because a thing done at night is a thing nobody can check. *I am not going to be the first man in this district to sell a night, and if I did there would not be a book in this fen afterwards that could do one thing about it, and I would have taken twelve pounds for it, and the book would have been right* | Ch 75 |
| **The name he did not give** | **one of the two men buys on the Ashcombe index and Renner knows his name and would not say it in a book, and the reason is entered in his own words: if I say his name in your book, then your book is a thing somebody can take off me.** The clerk entered that a man declined to name a buyer and gave his reason, the body accepted it, and **Anneke Roos initialled as an opinion, faster than any in a season, a standing of this holding: a man may decline to name a buyer in a book this body keeps; the clerk enters that he declined and why; the body does not ask again and does not enter the name if it is told one. The clerk added that nobody in the yard is to go and find it out and come back with it** | Ch 75 |
| **How many collars exist** | **four made by Renner in eleven years — two given away, one sold, one lost, and the one lost is the one in this yard — and a fifth man in this district who has one and whom Renner has not asked and does not intend to ask, entered on the page so that nobody can say afterward that it was not.** Four remain in the Fennmere yard and he is not selling them | Ch 75 |
| **Form RW/3, paid** | **Renner filled in his own copy on the sixth and sent it to a holding on the Marrowfield road that cannot afford a cooperative, with the line at the foot written out in his own hand and his name on it as the licensed man, and told the fen there is no compost in that holding and wrote that down too — and told the fen first, on the tenth, because the fen refused it and he did not have to.** This is the form coming back filled in by another hand, half a season early | Ch 75 |
| **The district's answer of four lines** | **written to the district on the eleventh about the offer and answered on the Thursday cart: a licensed man's private arrangement with a purchaser is not a matter for the district; the district examines a man on a working and not on a paper; there is no form; a person may write to the Assembly and the Assembly will examine him and will not tell the person what it found.** No copy in Cray and cannot be. **A form for the water, a form for the seed, a form for a form, and not one form for a man being offered twelve pounds to cut a collar on a Sunday, and that is not because anybody is hiding anything** | Ch 75 |
| **The two walks** | **Jonas Byre went to two of the eleven holdings on Renner's price list on the eleventh with the offer and the refusal in his coat. The first heard him out, thanked him, asked what his standing was, was told he is a member of a cooperative eleven miles off and his license is not a license for anything, and shut the door. The second did not open the door, had heard about the collar, wanted to know what it cost, and is thinking about it.** Both entered with the hour at the well house door | Ch 75 |
| **The C/4 answer** | **down the March road on the twelfth, twenty-eight days after the last day of the fourteen this holding worked out for itself, in a canvas bag with a seal broken and mended twice. A one-page covering letter over a man Tova Reed named once, and nine sheets, produced late, in full, with a schedule of four things the trust cannot produce and the reason in each case.** One of the four is a column | Ch 76 |
| **The trust's eleventh column** | **the trust's crop histories are on sheets with eleven printed columns and the seventh is *source*, and in eleven years it has never held anything but a place. There is no column for cuttings, none for a line dispersed to ground and not returned from, none for a steward. The answer says so in one line: the trust cannot produce a record of cuttings dispatched, there being no column for it, and no such record was made, and this is not an omission from the answer but the absence of the thing itself** | Ch 76 |
| **The March road** | **out at two places and out since the fourteenth: the first a mile short of the low dry, where the ground has gone, passable with an empty bed and not returnable loaded; the second further down and the bad one. The trust cannot move a crate north or south until the season turns, and has asked in the same line whether this holding would hold the trust's seed for one season. The holding would, cannot pay carriage, and was not asked to** | Ch 76 |
| **The trust's sentence, which is the volume's answer said by a body nine hundred miles off** | *A line kept in one book is a line that can be got at. A line kept in eleven grounds and no book is a line that can be sold. The trust has not been paid for the cuttings and does not know that they were paid for and has now entered that it does not know, and the trust's position is that this is a fault in the trust and not in any holder, and that a fault of that kind cannot be corrected by paying anybody, and can only be corrected by saying it out loud in a book that anybody may read. The trust understands that the trust has said so in one* | Ch 76 |
| **The cutting list is now in two books in two countries** | **Tova Reed wrote the eleven names to the trust on the ninth of next month, three days after telling the fen, and they went with the copy of the fen's book because the annex's fourth term sends a copy of everything to Cray. The eleventh name is this holding, which is a party to a charter condition. One list, two hands, no private copy anywhere — and it is the first time in eleven years that has been true of anything she has done, and it also means the fen can now be asked for it** | Ch 76 |
| **The map** | **the district wrote for it on the thirteenth, one sheet, the office's and not Hilary Skeath's, asking for the Crown seed-route sheet in the version current at the last revision together with any marginal annotation of this holding's own making, for a schedule check under the charter. It went on the Thursday cart with the printing, with a second sheet of a page and a half, answered inside four days.** The second sheet names the ninth legend mark, says the branch is used for the supply of eleven households and is a local holder in fact, makes no claim to the Crown's layer, says the mark is not a warning to the holder but a handover to whoever is not the holder, and notes that this holding is the holder and was not on the sheet when the sheet was printed and the sheet has not been reprinted in nine years and nobody appears to have been required to say anything about it | Ch 76 |
| **Tova Reed's map decision, and the question it opens** | **she gave the map away and kept the record, and the two are different things and only one of them is anybody's to give away twice.** Her own last line on the return, entered in the reader's hand and going to the district with a copy staying in the fen: *a person who drew that mark knew that the line was maintained and did not know or did not care who maintained it; this holding has now said out loud, in a return to a district, that it is a holder of a line the map says has none, and somebody in an office is now going to have to decide what to be done about a holder that appeared, and this holding has no idea what the answer is and has not asked to be told, and it wants it entered that the question was opened on the twenty-fourth of last month by a district officer who did not open it* | Ch 76 |
| **The letter from the dry end** | **three lines, on the afternoon post of the fourteenth, on foot, by a woman who walked eleven days, giving her name and no house: she keeps seed for eleven households on ground the district says it maintains and does not; she had this holding's eleven years copied out of a sheet a buyer read out loud in a market two hundred miles from here; she will pay a shilling a copy to anybody who has one to sell; the road is out at two places.** Entered as received from a person not named in this holding's book. A boy went down the fen road with the letter and a copy and a shilling of the fen's money | Ch 76 |
| **The return line, in full, on the fourteenth** | **nothing put into the return main this month, none available, for the second month running. The shortfall three hundred and ninety-nine hundredweight, seventy-nine pounds sixteen at four shillings, carried forward and not discharged. The bed at three and a half degrees. The March's tin unmoved, eighty-five days at risk. The leak at five-eighths, unmoved under a full draw, not mended. The beat logged eleven times. Nineteen acres standing, the Longneck at eleven degrees, four willow sets alive, the graft fed about a twentieth.** The charter expires in fifteen days with no appeal anywhere in the four clauses, and Ruben Ashe is named in this holding's own book as returning at that date | Ch 76 |

## The section-nine margin notes: the twelfth and the thirteenth, and the counting

**The chain is now thirteen and the counting has been checked.** Eleven at the end of Batch 0002 → **twelve** in Ch 74 (the seventh) → **thirteen** in Ch 76 (the fourteenth). Both are signed by nobody but Marek and both are read back before they are initialled.

1. **The twelfth, Ch 74, the seventh.** A collar of living wood bought from a licensed man at two shillings and the four seconds and what it does and does not say; a certificate of origin on a house's letterhead; an offer refused in a yard by seven hands of which two were against and one of the two was the engineer of record; two hundred pages set in print and sold at a shilling a copy; eleven of the eleven cuttings entered in two books in two countries with no private copy; the district's demand for a Crown sheet answered inside four days with a second sheet naming this holding a holder of a line the Crown sheet says has none; a licensed man offered twelve pounds for a collar on a Sunday and refusing in writing and coming up the road five days before his own date to say so; a district that has no form for any of it; and twenty-two days.
2. **The thirteenth, Ch 76, the fourteenth.** The trust's answer twenty-eight days late with the schedule of four things it cannot produce and the reason in each case; the eleventh column and the absence of the thing itself; the March road out at two places; the map given away with a counter-mark; two hundred pages off the press and forty copies and one sold in the yard; the letter from a person who gave no house and walked eleven days; and fifteen days.

**The closing sentence, *This is worth nothing against a cut*, stands on eleven of the thirteen notes and is off the sixth and the eighth only.** That is the same pattern as at the end of Batch 0002 — two notes without it, both records and not arguments, both saying so underneath in the engineer's own hand — and **no later writer may add a note that carries the sentence without saying whether it is a record or an argument, and may not remove one.**

## The Fieldbook panel: one spent, in Chapter 73, and the allowance

**Spent, once, in Chapter 73, on the sixth, at the eleventh hour, at the seed-house table, with the instrument laid against the wood of the bead in a copper collar.** Coll Renner is the licensed man and fitted his own clip; the contact was tested with a plain wire gauge and the reading of the test written down first in Renner's own hand; **Lissa Vale wrote the use-log line first, on the page, and read it back, and Marek read it back after her, and it went in as the eleventh line** and records the contact, the hour, the roll and nothing whatever about what came out. It is set as a block quotation of three plain lines in the volume's house format:

> The ring reads the last hand.
>
> It does not read the ground, and it never will.
>
> There is nothing here a buyer may not buy.

**It cost what it costs.** Eleven seconds he cannot account for, as with the four on a cut face and the eleven at the head; the four inches on his forearm hot before it was over and staying hot; his hand from the wrist down no use to him from the second hour; Renner holding the case down the seed-house steps.

**Nothing in this batch quotes the three lines in a document, and Lissa Vale refuses to enter them on the rule she has now given out loud twice.** Renner asks for that and then tells the room what entering them would cost him — any man with a wet cloth could read them off a collar in a market, nine carriers would sell for every line, and he could not say where it was published, because it would be in this fen's book. **So the fen decides the batch's central question without the one instrument that might have settled it, and the instrument tells them the problem and not the answer, which is what it has done twice before.**

**A deliberate decision a reviewer should know about: Renner says all three lines out loud in the yard, once, to the six people in the room.** This is not the panel entering a document and it is not a person finding it; it is the licensed man who made the collar repeating it to a room that already knows the whole of the problem, and it is the smallest possible instance of the harm he describes two paragraphs later. The rule on the page is that no line enters a book, and the rule holds, and **a later writer may not have the three lines appear in any document, on any form, in any letter, in any printing, or in any of Wrenlow's records, and may not have a character quote them as authority.**

**The Volume 02 allowance is now zero for Batch 0003, and one panel per batch is available to Batches 0004 and 0005.**

## The use log, and where it stands

**The use log is eleven lines from Chapter 73, and it now governs the collar as well as the instrument and the fieldbook.** A collar is a bead of living rootwood in a ring of copper, and putting one against a wetted lot is a contact that cannot be checked, and Lissa Vale said on the twenty-fifth that a wet cloth is not a contact and then wrote the lines anyway. **A thing which cannot be checked is a thing that has to be counted.**

1. the tenth, two months back, entered on the eleventh, the lateness declared. 2. the twentieth, the same month. 3. the fourth of last month, a refusal with the reason against it. 4. the seventeenth, two months back, at four in the morning, on the back of the page. 5. the thirtieth of last month, a bead, a clip, a license and a witness. 6. the twelfth of this month, the instrument on the head of the node at the eleventh hour, licensed man present, contact tested first, **and nothing else whatever.** 7. **the twenty-fifth**, the fen's own bean and the March's wet-line rye, two lots, two collar contacts, four seconds each, **and the two results that were places.** 8. **the twenty-fifth**, the grafted seedling, one collar contact, **and the reading not entered, with Lissa Vale's nine-line refusal entered with it.** 9. **the twenty-eighth**, a collar in a copper ring against a wetted lot, a licensed man not required and not present, the lot the property of a house at Marrowfield, four seconds, **and nothing at all about what the four seconds were.** 10. **the third of next month**, the ninth hour, thirty-one trays and nine tins on a floor, one collar, **and no findings.** 11. **the sixth**, the eleventh hour, the instrument against the wood of a bead in a collar, licensed man present and named and the roll named, contact tested before the contact and the test written down first, **and nothing whatever about what came out.**

**There is still no use log for the fieldbook, there never has been, and a later chapter may not supply one or propose one.** The stranger of the twenty-second of last month wrote that absence down in a notebook two hundred miles away and it has not been quoted since.

## Stage and contact accounting after Volume 02 Batch 0003

- **Marek is Stage 2, Grafthand, and this batch does not move him.** The stage is one small graft old. He can make a small reliable graft between living tissue and a limited local branch and he can keep two or three active links; a fourth fails. **He still cannot draw, has no bead, no anchor work, no bridge above a local conduit, no seedheart, no caretaker link and no reading of the deep archive, and the touchroot sense is unchanged and still unreliable.** He **laid a collar on a wetted lot four times in this batch and felt four seconds through the copper each time**, which is a contact with a device and not a graft and is not Stage 3 and may not be counted as a stage. **No number in this batch may be used to move him.**
- **The rootmark is present and unchanged: four inches along the inside of the left forearm, branching twice, permanent, recorded in this holding's book by Coll Renner under his own hand in Chapter 57.** In this batch it was hot before the Ch 73 contact was over and stayed hot, and the hand from the wrist down was no use to him from the second hour of that afternoon. **Nobody in this yard wrote the word *became* and nobody may.**
- **Contacts behind him: the four of Volume 01, unchanged in kind and cost; one at the head of the node in Ch 63 entered as a contact and not a finding and not a fifth contact in the Volume 01 sense; and one in Ch 73, the instrument against the wood of a collar bead, eleven seconds, at a licensed man's hands with a licensed man's clip, cost the mark and a day of the hand, and producing three lines that cannot be checked and cannot be entered.** The four collar readings of a wetted lot are device contacts and are logged in the use log and are not rootwork contacts on the node.
- **No draw and no return by rootlight in this batch**, and the panel's second line is the reason the volume does not need one: *it does not read the ground, and it never will.*

## New locks from the drafted Volume 02 Batch 0003

- **A device that makes provenance self-certifying is a device that makes provenance ownable, and the two are the same mechanism.** The mechanism is stated on the page twice and in three forms: a graft keeps the thing put in and loses the thing that was there, so the device answers the last graft; a lot nobody has chosen reports where it was grown, and a lot anybody has chosen reports who chose it; and the certificate's third line, *keeper of record*, is the one line no device can fill in and is filled in by the seller. **A later chapter may not have a market, a buyer, a printer or a district turn this into a paradox or a moral, and may not have anybody say the word *provocateur* about it.**
- **The dry-land line of the Dryfoot Trust is not lost.** It was cut and dispersed to eleven holdings in the eleventh month two seasons back, one of which is this fen, and it is in no book, and every record that said it was lost is wrong. **This is a change to a standing fact of two volumes and it is recorded here as a proposed canon change and not applied silently.** The seed house of Lantern Reach and the trust's counting house both carried *the dry-land line is still lost* in eleven-year books, and Tova Reed has now entered the correction in both and in a printing and on the road to Cray, and it cannot be withdrawn.
- **Nobody in Tarrow Fen proved where the Marrowfield cutting came from and nobody ever will from a book.** The certificate is true, the lot is the March's dry line, and the only fact in the chain is a shed and a man, and the buyer has not looked at a cutting sheet in twenty-two years because there has never been a column for it. **The shape of the volume's question is on the page and the content is nowhere, and this batch has added a person, a list of eleven names and a shed, and a person and a shed are not a map and a printer and a reason.**
- **A refusal that is entered in a book becomes a document, and this time it is a document other people pay for.** The Marrowfield offer was entered in full in the fen's book and is now a thing a printer may read and a copy may be made of, exactly as the Ashcombe lot's certificate was a thing a buyer's house could act on. **Being readable is a position and not a virtue and no later chapter may convert it into a compliment.**
- **A man may decline to name a buyer in a book this holding keeps, and the holding does not ask again and does not enter the name if it is told one.** Anneke Roos's standing, initialled as an opinion faster than any in a season. **A later chapter may not have anybody go and find out.**
- **The fen has a district that has no form.** A licensed man's private arrangement with a purchaser, a certificate of origin, a market in collars, and a Sunday shift are all outside every instrument in this district, and the district's answer is correct and unhelpful and is entered as such. **No villain may be inserted to fix this and no office may be made to lie, obstruct or act corruptly.**
- **The third of the three conditions in clause three will be described at the first examination held after the twenty-ninth of the month after next, and will be tested on whoever holds the node on that day.** It is not tested before the term ends because no condition is tested twice in the same term and the term ends with the charter. **This is now the single hardest thing in the volume and it is a form, and no later chapter may name it, soften it, or have anybody in Tarrow Fen guess it.**
- **The Charter's own rules are the resource the volume has left, and they are rules, not weapons.** A thing done at night is a thing nobody can check; a name in a book anybody may read is a name anybody may use; a thing only the six of us read is a diary. **Three licensed men and one licensed contractor have now quoted this holding's own rules back at it in its own yard, and a later chapter may not have the rules be wrong.**
- **The printing exists, forty copies, and it cannot be recalled.** It is a two-hundred-page record of eleven years including the largest wrong any of them has entered, and a copy went to Cray, a copy went to Marrowfield, one was sold in the yard, and one went down the fen road to a woman with no house who walked eleven days for it. **The dry-land line's absence is now a printed fact and the fen's own copying of a name off a Crown list is on the front page, and neither of those can be unprinted.**
- **Nothing romantic has happened and nothing may lean that way.** Tova Reed gave a book of two hundred pages out of another body's money, entered the largest wrong of her career against that body's interest, refused a lease on her own record, and asked Marek Vale to be the man who said it out loud in a yard. **That is the whole of it and it is institutional, and no later chapter may make it anything else.**
- **Lissa Vale is not forgiven and is not thanked.** She refused a reading in nine lines and the book has a hole in it because of her, she put her hand up first and would not say which way, she read out the fen's own keeper's confession because she is the reader and the reader reads, and nobody in that yard said a word to her about the ninth tray. **Her refusal of the fourth of a month of Batch 0002 is hers, is not Sera Quill's, is not her own fourth, and is not numbered.**
- **Kellan Rusk is not in this batch and is not mentioned except in the arithmetic of the eighty a day, and the word *became* and the word *redeemed* are both prohibited for him in this volume.** His breach stands disclosed in his own words beside the figure and not below it.
- **Nia is not in this batch.** Not in a room, not in a letter, not by report, and nobody may say she decided not to deliver the letter. **The letter is in the open drawer of the seed-house table, sealed, and it has now been unopened and undiscussed across four batches and about sixty days of season, and no chapter in this batch wrote down why, and that is the standing.**
- **Sera Quill is not in this batch, is not asked about Brinewake, and nobody has asked about her fourth refusal.** It is unnumbered and it may not be numbered. **The volume's first licensed witness has now been Coll Renner, at a load test the district ordered, and it is still not Sera Quill and no chapter may have her simply arrive.**
- **The seal is still a leak, five-eighths, unmoved under a full draw, unmended, undiagnosed, and not a mystery.** Nobody wrote underneath it. **The panel did not diagnose it and the test did not diagnose it and a later chapter may not make either one of them do it.**
- **The beat is logged eleven times and is undiagnosed.** It did not change under a full draw, and that fact is entered and **nothing was written underneath it about what it means.**
- **The bypass is unbuilt and unfunded. Ivo Tarrant's four men do not come back and are not a rescue.** The Thornwild settlement is not named, the long line is not used, quoted or entered, and the word *seat* does not appear anywhere in Chapters 69–76. Marda Vole, Dunmar's *none known*, and the sealed envelope are untouched and none of them may be undone.

## Proposed canon changes recorded, not silently applied — Volume 02 Batch 0003

Batch 0003 added nine things by drafting. All are local detail, mechanisms, forms, prices, places, people or records, and none changes a world rule, a power rule, a relationship lock, or the ending:

1. **The dry-land line is not lost.** Standing across Volume 01 and Volume 02 to date: *the dry-land line is still lost* / *the first row of the March's own line is thinner by fourteen pounds ten* / *the March's tin at risk and the dry-land line is still lost.* The manuscript now says the line was dispersed to eleven holdings in the eleventh month two seasons back, that the eleventh is this fen, and that the statement that it is lost is a wrong entered by Tova Reed in three books, in a printing, and on the road to Cray. **A later writer may not have the line be lost, may not have it come back quietly, and may not have it be paid for or recovered; it exists in eleven unnamed grounds and the fen knows one of them and the other ten are not in any book.** The correction is entered and irreversible.
2. **The seed house's own name came off a crate as well as off a Crown list.** Lissa Vale's four-line answer of the eighteenth of last month — the seed house does not know, has never known, copied its own name off a Crown list in a year it does not name — is **untouched and still true of the fen's ledger.** Tova Reed's ninth line adds that the name was written by her at Cray eleven years ago in a season when an unestablished name would not have been sown. **Both halves stand: the fen copied the name and the trust wrote it, and neither of them knew what it was, and the device now reports the writing hand rather than the ground.**
3. **The certificate of origin** — three printed lines, *ground*, *season*, *keeper of record* — is a new document class in the volume and is a trade form, not a Crown form. It is on a private letterhead, it costs four shillings, and **the volume's discovery is that the device fills two of the three lines and the seller fills the third.**
4. **A lot of one part in eleven is a legal sale and the trade has agreed to call it one**, and Tova Reed's sentence saying so is set on the last page of the printing as a notice and not an opinion. **This is a new trade rule and it is legal by agreement and not by statute, and a later chapter may not make it illegal and may not make a Crown act it.**
5. **The eleventh column of the trust's crop histories is *source* and it has never held anything but a place, and there is no column for cuttings, none for a steward, and none for a line dispersed and not returned.** A whole class of record is missing because no form was printed for it, which is the volume's own argument in a table.
6. **The third of the three conditions in clause three** is now given a test date and a test holder by a district form, and is still not described. This is a new lock and a new forward pull and it is the shape of the volume's resolution, not its content.
7. **The map counter-mark** — a page and a half sent to a district, naming this holding a holder of a line the Crown sheet says has none, making no claim to the Crown's layer — is a new instrument class and a new kind of act, and it is correct, dated, and cannot be withdrawn. **It is the bridge premise and the next volume's question in one sheet.**
8. **Ottoline Fray, of Fray and Daughters at Marrowfield** — about forty-one, four generations, forty-one smallholders, twenty-two years, a collar bought in the spring, a winter list, a buyer in the low dry. **She is not a member of the cooperative and no new named member of the cooperative was created in this batch**: the crew is still Ilsa Kern, Jonas Byre, Anneke Roos, Gael Senn, Lissa Vale and Dunmar Fitch, plus the man of about fifty and the man of about seventy, who are held by function and may not be given names without recording it. **She is not redeemed and not villained, she pays a shilling a copy for the printing, and she is the volume's first customer who wants the record and not the device.**
9. **A letter from a person who gave a name and no house**, eleven households, ground the district says it maintains and does not, eleven days on foot, a shilling a copy to anybody who has one to sell. **A settlement at the dry end has now written to this fen once and nothing about it has been named, and Movement 4 owns it and may name it, and this batch does not.**

None of the nine changes a world rule, a power rule, a relationship lock, or the ending. **The ending is untouched: Chancellor Iona Vey remains the final antagonist, the Rootway is not a mind, and the volume's question — who altered the maps and the seed records enough to make a bridge between this fen and the dry Salt March legal, and who was paid for the alteration — is still unanswered.** This batch has added to it a person, a list of eleven names, a shed at Marrowfield, a certificate that is true, a column that does not exist, and a map mark that was drawn nine years ago by somebody who knew the line was maintained. **None of those is a map, a printer and a reason, and the shape is on the page and the content is nowhere, which is where it was before this batch and is now a great deal more legible and no more answered.**

# VOLUME 02 — BATCH 0004, CHAPTERS 77–88, MOVEMENT 4

**Volume 02, *The Graft Market*. Twelve chapters, Movement 4, *The Bridge*. The volume's outline is `outline/volume-02.md`. Its governing prompt is `workspace/volume-02/batch-0004/PROMPT.md`, which was written from the outline and the state files in the phase that planned the batch and carries the twelve cards. Volume 01 is closed. Volume 02 Batches 0001, 0002 and 0003 are closed. Nothing below reopens any of them.**

## Volume 02 Batch 0004 day clock, confirmed against the drafted chapters

Ch 77 days 105–106 (**Sat the fifteenth, Sun the sixteenth**) · Ch 78 days 107–108 (**Mon the seventeenth, Tue the eighteenth**) · Ch 79 days 109–110 (Wed the nineteenth, Thu the twentieth) · Ch 80 days 111–112 (Fri the twenty-first, Sat the twenty-second) · Ch 81 days 113–114 (**Sun the twenty-third, Mon the twenty-fourth**) · Ch 82 days 115–116 (Tue the twenty-fifth, Wed the twenty-sixth) · Ch 83 days 117–118 (Thu the twenty-seventh, Fri the twenty-eighth) · Ch 84 days 119–120 (**Sat the twenty-ninth, Sun the thirtieth**) · Ch 85 days 121–122 (**Mon the first of next month, Tue the second**) · Ch 86 days 123–124 (Wed the third, Thu the fourth) · Ch 87 days 125–126 (Fri the fifth, **Sat the sixth**) · Ch 88 days 127–129 (**Sun the seventh, Mon the eighth, Tue the ninth**).

**One chapter reaches one day past its own range, deliberately, and it is Ch 87.** Its last three paragraphs — the copies going up the fen road, the closing entry read aloud in the yard twice, and the man of about fifty's last sentence in the batch — are all in the past tense about **the seventh (day 127)**, and Ch 88 opens on the same seventh and does not repeat any of them. **Day 127 therefore belongs to the close of Ch 87 and the head of Ch 88, and it must not be counted twice in any reconciliation of the batch.** The entry it describes is the first entry in this holding's book about something a person at the far end of a route was asked to do, and a reader who has read it must not be told it happened again in Ch 88.

**The batch runs days 105–129 with no gap and no overlap. Day 104 was the fourteenth of the month that began on day 91, a Friday, which is Batch 0003's last day. The batch crosses one month boundary: days 105–120 are the fifteenth through the thirtieth of the month that began on day 91, and days 121–129 are the first through the ninth of the next. Every weekday follows from day 61 = the first = a Thursday, locked in Volume 01 Chapter 49.** The three weekday statements a reviewer may check the chain against are **the charter expiring at midnight on a Saturday, day 119, and the district holding its sittings on Saturdays**; **the first of next month being a Monday, day 121**; and **the convoy moving on a Saturday, day 126, because a convoy moves when carts go**.

### The dating rule in this range, and it is the Volume 02 rule with one thing added

Days 105–120 are in the month that began on day 91, which is **two months back from day 121**. Days 121–129 are in the month that began on day 121. Therefore, inside a scene on day 121 or later, **day 119 is *the twenty-ninth of last month* and never *the twenty-ninth***, and day 91 is *the first of last month*. Inside a scene on day 105 to day 120, a bare ordinal is unambiguous and the batch uses it. **No month is named anywhere in Chapters 77–88 and no document in the batch carries one.** Four calendar month names appeared in the first draft and were cut during the drafting pass: *March* (a carter's man's "not in March"), *February* (a clause-two argument), *November* (Tova Reed on the district notice) and *April* (the man of about fifty on the seventh column). **October is four months back by day 105 and is named nowhere, and the volume's own idiom for it, established in Batch 0003, is *two months back*.** One idiom appears twice and is a number and not a name: *the eleventh month two seasons back*, which is how Tova Reed's own cutting-list heading has read all along.

### The dating pass, and the seventeen places it was wrong, and every one of them fixed

**The table above is the chain. The first draft's chapter-level dating was wrong in seventeen places, all of them the same class of error, and all seventeen were found and fixed in a single sweep after the batch was finished. They are recorded here item by item because a later writer needs to know that the class of error is still live in this range and that the batch passed its own audit rather than being born clean.**

**The rule the sweep applied.** There are five months in play: days 1–30, 31–60, 61–90, 91–120 and 121–150. **One warning before the table: every *Now* value in it is read against the scene that says it and not against the rule for day 105.** The Ch 85 and Ch 86 rows are scenes on days 121–124, where days 31–60 are *three* months back and days 91–120 are *last month* — so **Ch 86's *the twelfth* is day 102 and is correctly *the twelfth of last month*, while the same ordinal in a Chapter 77–80 scene would be day 72 and mean something else entirely.** A reader who applies one rule down the whole table will get that row wrong in both directions. **Inside a scene on days 105–120, days 91–120 are *this month*, days 61–90 are *last month*, days 31–60 are *two months back* and days 1–30 are *three months back*. Inside a scene on days 121–129, days 121–150 are *this month*, days 91–120 are *last month*, days 61–90 are *two months back*, days 31–60 are *three months back* and days 1–30 are *four months back*.** Every instance of *of last month*, *of this month* and *of a month two/three/four months back* in Chapters 77–88 was checked against that table and the following were wrong on the page and are now right:

| Ch | Day | Was | Now | What it dated |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 77 | 105 | *two months back* | *three months back* | the spent bark running out at the middle of a month, which is day 15 |
| 77 | 105 | *two months back* | *last month* | the compost bay's making, and the trust's question of the twelfth |
| 77 | 105 | *two months back* | *three months back* | the ledger going on the middle of the table, now the fourth, matching Batch 0003's own usage of that date |
| 78 | 107 | *last month* | *this month* | the letter of the fourteenth |

The notice of the eighteenth is referenced and not reproduced anywhere in the manuscript; no later chapter may date it differently.** |

**Two other counts were corrected in the same sweep.** The compost bay's number was described as *the number of days it has stood empty* and is now *the number of days the bay has stood there*, with the note that there has never been a difference, which makes thirty-nine on the fifteenth and fifty-eight on the fourth of next month both true against a bay made on about the sixty-sixth. And **Perrin Osse's *in nineteen days* is now *in a season*,** because there is no nineteenth day in the interval the sentence was sitting in.

**Two distances in the batch are load-bearing and were checked twice each.** The two days of the Wrenlow visit are the twenty-seventh and the twenty-eighth, out and back, with a night at a bothy. And the interval between the return of the fourteenth of last month and the clerk's four points of the ninth is **twenty-five days**, and **this line was wrong twice and is now right**: the first version of this paragraph called the return *a month two months back*, called the interval **ninety-three days**, and pointed a reader at the sentence *four days over four months*. **The prose now says *four days over four weeks* (day 104 to day 129, with the form on day 111 and the schedule on day 115) and the sentence in the chapter agrees with it.** A writer who quotes this paragraph must quote this version and not the earlier one.
