- **The word *eleven* stands at fifty-seven and was sixty.** Three inert instances were varied, all in Ch 123: **the first winter's ten weeks, not eleven, in the arithmetic and once more beside it; and the naming standing's thirteen words, not eleven.** **Every remaining instance is a separate standing** — the eleven cuts, the eleven words of the two refusals, the eleven minutes of the removal, the eleven people in a yard, the eleventh hour, the eleventh of the month, the eleven shillings, the eleven years of a practice, the eleven hours of salt flat — **and a later writer may not vary any of them.** **A separate interval was corrected in the same pass and is not an eleven: Ch 127's *three sentences in two months* is not in a fortnight, because the first of the three was given on the fifth of a month two months back.**
- **Section-nine chain:** thirty-second (Ch 120, the fourteenth), thirty-third (Ch 122, the eighteenth), thirty-fourth (Ch 124, the twenty-second), thirty-fifth (Ch 127, the twenty-eighth), thirty-sixth (Ch 128, the thirtieth). Consecutive, sentence off the thirty-second only. **Ch 125 has no note and the reason is entered underneath it.** One day is shared between no two chapters and no chapter reaches past its own range.
- **Volume 03 stands at 28,582 + 28,816 + 29,496 = 86,894 words across thirty chapters, and the manuscript at 365,238 + 29,496 = 394,734 across 128 chapters.** Volume 02's 141,818 and Volume 01's 166,022 both stand.

# VOLUME 03 — BATCH 0004, CHAPTERS 129–138, MOVEMENT 4

**Volume 03, *The Memory Orchard*, Movement 4, *The Unfit Category*. Ten chapters, days 211–230. The volume's outline is `outline/volume-03.md`. Its governing prompt is `workspace/volume-03/batch-0004/PROMPT.md`. Volume 01 and Volume 02 are closed. Nothing below reopens an earlier volume and nothing below moves a date the Batch 0001, 0002 or 0003 pass settled.**

## The dating rule inside this batch, which is the eighth month and only one naming table

Days 211–240 are the eighth month, and the whole batch sits inside it, so there is no month turn inside these ten chapters and one naming table, as in Batch 0003. Day 210 was the thirtieth of the seventh and is Batch 0003's last day. **Day 211 is the first of the eighth month and a Sunday, which follows from day 210 being a Saturday. Day 231 is the twenty-first of the eighth and is outside this batch and is Batch 0005's first day.**

| Day | Named, in Chapters 129–138 |
|---|---|
| 1–30 | *a month eight months back* |
| 31–60 | *a month seven months back* |
| 61–90 | *a month six months back* |
| 91–120 | *a month five months back* |
| 121–150 | *a month four months back* |
| 151–180 | *a month three months back* |
| 181–210 | *last month* |
| 211–230 | *of this month*, *the first*, *the second*, or the bare ordinal |

**October is the first month, is eight months back by day 211, and is named nowhere.** *March* is not a month; the only capitalized *March* in the ten chapters is *the March's tin*.

## Volume 03 Batch 0004 day clock, confirmed against the drafted chapters

Ch 129 day 211 (**Sun the first**) · Ch 130 days 212–213 (Mon the second, Tue the third) · Ch 131 days 214–215 (Wed the fourth, Thu the fifth) · Ch 132 days 216–217 (Fri the sixth, Sat the seventh) · Ch 133 days 218–219 (**Sun the eighth, Mon the ninth**) · Ch 134 days 220–221 (Tue the tenth, Wed the eleventh) · Ch 135 days 222–223 (Thu the twelfth, Fri the thirteenth) · Ch 136 days 224–225 (**Sat the fourteenth, Sun the fifteenth**) · Ch 137 days 226–227 (Mon the sixteenth, Tue the seventeenth) · Ch 138 days 228–230 (Wed the eighteenth, Thu the nineteenth, **Fri the twentieth**).

**The batch runs days 211–230 with no gap and no chapter reaches past its own range, and no day is shared between two chapters.** Day 231 is outside it.

**The four weekday statements on the page that a reviewer may check, and all four resolve:** the seventh month's compost line entered on the first of the eighth month, a **Sunday**; the instrument served in person at the north gate on the second, a **Monday**; the question sheet read back in a yard on the sixteenth, a **Monday**; and the standing figures of the month read at about the seventh hour of the twentieth, a **Friday**.

**Staging.** Chs 129–133, 135, 136 and 137 are at Lantern Reach. The shelf is narrated from outside in four places, all of them documents or paid messengers, which is the Batch 0002 and 0003 rule and the reason the rule exists: **Ch 129 the day letter looked at on the first; Ch 133–134 the question sent up and the four houses' answer; Ch 137 the ordinary fourth-hour reading of the well on the sixteenth; Ch 138 the growth on the sixteenth and the sheet that came down on the twentieth.** **No character is on the shelf and back at the fen inside ten days. The plateau road is four days up and four days down and no chapter may make it shorter; the nine days up and four down elsewhere in this file are the four-person cart expedition of Ch 112, which is a different journey, and they may never be used for a hired man.**

## Numbers and records established in Chapters 129–138

| Fact | Value | Established |
|---|---|---|
| **The seventh month's compost line** | **nothing put in, none available, specification four hundred hundredweight, shortfall four hundred and two hundredweight, eighty pounds eight at four shillings the hundredweight, carried forward and not discharged, run of seven months not discharged in seven.** Entered on the first of the eighth by the examiner, in the steward's book, in a hand that is not the steward's, **reader the man of about fifty.** Fifty hundredweight better than the sixth month, the reason being that two long journeys (thirteen weeks of horse, and the descent of the plateau road on the twenty-seventh of a month three months back) are twenty-six hundredweight each and are not in it | Ch 129 |
| **The zero** | **entered for the first time in this body's history, as a figure and not a sentence.** A hundred and forty-one trees on a shelf, read and cleaned and dated, and not one hundredweight of anything has ever come back to this holding. **It is entered as not a loss, not a fault, and not a claim, and the fen has no standing over a hundred and forty-one trees** | Ch 129 |
| **The day letter, looked at on the first** | four houses, one drawing each in turn, first house a Tuesday, on a stone at the well at the fourth house since the sixth of last month. **Days, twenty-eight. Pails carried up, counted by each house in its own pail: two thousand and ninety-six.** Four pails, not the same pail. **The figure is a household's and the fen cannot check it and cannot reduce it, and no person in this fen can say how much water it is** | Ch 129, 137 |
| **The not-being-there** | entered with the hour. **This body was not at the well at Cauldron Reach on the first of this month.** The choice was the compost line over the shelf, the choice was not made in a yard, and the reason is in the fen's own words: **a body cannot be in two places on the first of a month and the seventh month of a run does not wait for a road.** Not entered as correct and not entered as a fault | Ch 129 |
| **The instrument, served in person** | the fourth part of the schedule of records held, **served at the north gate on the second of this month, in person, by Sabin Roell, a records officer of the district, about thirty-four**, met at the foot of the plateau road and not at a gate, her name given once and not taken down a second time, and the fen entered that she was not entered twice because she is already in the book. **Production at the office at the foot of the plateau on the fourteenth of this month.** Three things in her order: the form, then the line, then **the fact that she cannot take the line off and is not able to** | Ch 130 |
| **The line** | **where the holder does not state a part within the time stated, the record is held to be in such class as this office determines, and that class is entered against the record and not against the holder, and the holder is notified of it after it has been entered.** And the man of about fifty's reading: **we answered the form in seven days four months ago, put *held* against six items and classified nothing, and the office wrote that *held* is a fact about a holder and not about a record, and that answer is now the answer that puts a class on our records without a word from anybody. Silence was never a refusal. Silence was never anything at all until a line said what it is** | Ch 130 |
| **Who loses, twice** | **this body; and a woman on a shelf four hundred miles off whose ledger is a record of a household and is the only one there is, and this body has no standing over it and no power to state a part for it and no power to ask whether a part has been stated. This body did not write to her and decided not to.** Two lines, and the decision line is the one that matters | Ch 130 |
| **The four copies, sent again on the fourth** | **one, the ordinary post to Cray; two, the sub-office at Fennmere down the fen road with a covering line naming the office at the head of the form and asking nothing; three, a hired man on the plateau road; four, inside a licensed man's coat.** The sequence of two pages goes in the same four hands, in a **second bundle, tied with a second piece of string**, and is not on the return of the fourteenth. **The count of places is four, counted out loud with the names on them, and the fen is not nine places; three of the four are a building, a counter and a man, and the fourth is a man** | Ch 131 |
| **The fragment's description** | **goes nowhere. Two places: a drawer in the well house, and a person whose name is not entered.** The clerk was asked in a room for a third and entered that he could not find one, and entered that the word *so far* is not on the page. **The difference between the two counts and the four is a count of places and not of people, and a drawer is not a person and neither is a counting house** | Ch 131 |
| **Tova Reed's rule, entered whole** | **a readable thing survives in more places than one.** One place is a house, two is a habit, three is a body, four is a thing an office can get at and cannot get at twice in the same week. **And the rule says nothing about a thing that is not readable and does not apply to a woman on a shelf with a ledger, or to a piece of brass with a cut in it** | Ch 131 |
| **The argument against the copies** | **we are spreading a rumour of one in four places; a rumour that is true and a rumour that is not true cannot be told apart from outside a yard; a body that is in four places has four things to be wrong about and four that have to be right, and we cannot read four of them because we cannot read one.** It is still the right thing to do. **The reason this body is doing this is not that it believes in the truth. It is that it is afraid, and it has worked out that being in four places is the only shape of safety available to it, and that is a reason and not a principle.** Entered in his own hand and not improved on | Ch 131 |
| **The clerk's count of the fourth** | hands four, in two bundles; **roads two** (the fen road, nine miles, and the plateau road, four days); days out, one to Fennmere, six to Cray on the ordinary post, four to the shelf; **days on the road of the man, eight**; **four shillings on the fourth, the day he was hired, twenty-eight when he is paid off, the whole of the hire thirty-two, taken out of the compost line of this month anyway.** **People who now hold one of the four: one.** **People who could not be found with it, if it came to that: one — the man in the coat, who may be run over on this fen road in a month, after which this body holds three copies and two are in its own name and can be listed** | Ch 131 |
| **The class, named** | one sheet, a heading and a definition, four lines: **records of an experimental character, made in a working district, not confirmed by a second reading at the time of making, are held to be unfit for evidentiary use. A record so held may be produced by a holder, and is not to be produced by this office, and no officer of this district may be cited as a holder of one. The word *experimental* is not defined on this sheet and the office has stated that it does not define it, and that a holder who considers a record experimental may say so, and that a statement so made is itself a classification of the record and is entered against the record.** The man of about fifty: **we built a door and the door is a trapdoor and it was built by an office that is right, and the rightness is not going to be argued with by us in a yard** | Ch 132 |
| **What the class reaches** | three things, worked out on a slate and entered with the working and not only the answer: **one, a reading taken by a licensed man on a hired trestle in a stone barn on another district's ground, which is in this body's own book and is on the six; two, a copy of a page in a book in a locked room four hundred miles off, and nothing is said about whether that book is open; three, a maintenance order copied out by hand into the ledger of a household, and whether it exists is not known and was not asked and is not going to be asked, the reason being that this body decided on the second not to write to that household about a ledger** | Ch 132 |
| **The four walls, counted** | **the part three condition; the shape the schedule has no part for; the sealed record nobody can produce; and the class.** Four in three weeks, every one correct, **and not one of them built by this body.** Entered, and the fen is not proud of the count, and the count is the only thing it can do with them | Ch 132, 137 |
| **Coll Renner's problem, brought by himself** | **that sheet does not reach him. A class of record of an experimental character is held against the record and not against the holder, so it does not reach a person's coat. The one copy no office can get at is the one a man can lose.** He has had the coat on the fen road since the eighteenth of a month three months back, he has thought about it for three weeks, he has made no decision, he said it in a yard unasked, and he asked the clerk to enter that he said it and that nobody thanked him. **He is forty-one, has a boy of nine at Fennmere, and about nine months of the work of the most careful record in this district is the other thing that loses** (Lissa Vale, written in her own hand, read back by her, **no recommendation made and none asked for, and the line after it left blank**) | Ch 132, 137 |
| **Anneke Roos's sum in two halves** | **first half, and it can be done:** a hundred and forty-one trees, eleven acres, one harvest weighed at a barn door, **one hundred and nine hundredweight**, a fifth of it about **twenty-two hundredweight** set aside for four houses, at **nine shillings the hundredweight**, being the price this body heard said and not the price this body holds: **forty-nine pounds and one shilling.** **Second half:** the same trees, the same acres, at a price nobody has sent: **not held.** **The two halves were not added, not averaged, and not presented as one figure. The honest total is the first half and the two words.** The worst of it is not the number: **this holding has spent eleven years publishing every figure it has and has never once published a figure about what a person four hundred miles off is losing, and the reason is that it was not our money, and the reason it is doing it now is that an office has started a clock** | Ch 133 |
| **The question up the road** | sent on the ninth at the change of horses, one question, no advice, no figure: **are the rows going to be worked next season or not.** **This body did not tell a household what to do with a hundred and forty-one trees and asked them a question about their own ground, and those are different acts and only one of them is in the book, and the fen entered which one** | Ch 133 |
| **The rows let go** | on the tenth of this month, in daylight, in the yard at the first house, about nineteen people, about twenty minutes: **no feeding, no pruning, no selling, a hundred and forty-one trees of bearing left standing, the grass under them cut twice a year.** The reason is Merrid Ollvar's, nine sentences, not a virtue, given before anything was signed and written on the back of the reading, which is a practice of thirty-one years. The fen was not there, was not asked, did not know, and **the not-asking is entered and entered as correct** | Ch 134 |
| **The word nobody said** | available in that yard all day, used by nobody in it, not on the household's sheet, and **this body did not use it either, and a body four hundred miles off does not get to name a thing four houses did with their own ground** | Ch 134 |
| **Tova Reed's objection** | **a row of trees that nobody works is not a record and it is not a sacrifice until something happens in it**, and if nothing happens in it then four households have given up a year of fruit for nothing and this fen put the idea in their heads. **And a readable thing survives in more places than one is still true and she would say it again in front of a district officer tomorrow. A rule can be right and be the thing that does the damage, and I cannot tell those two apart from inside a rule** | Ch 134 |
| **Marek Vale's answer** | **I did not. They had a buyer who is gone and a paper they cannot be got at. I wrote nine lines they did not ask for and nine days too late and I said so in a yard four hundred miles off. If this was my doing it was done before I knew their fruit existed.** Then the order of the days: a question on the ninth, four days of road each way, an answer that **cannot** come down before the seventeenth, **and a body that could have sent a man and did not.** **Neither is a finding and both are in the book and he did not defend himself** | Ch 134 |
| **The second draw, attempted** | on the twelfth of this month, at a hard standing in the fen, in this holding's own low ground, from about the second hour of the afternoon to about the sixth, with a licensed man holding a lamp, a steward holding a page, the reader, the clerk and about nine people. **Water, heat and rootlight together, over about four feet, for about four hours, through a living path, and returned.** **Two links, both on a shelf four hundred miles off, and no link of his own in this yard; the path used was the fen's** | Ch 135 |
| **The standing rule, in full** | **one, the draw is not begun on ground whose return is not carrying; two, it is to be stopped at four feet; three, the stop is to be called out loud by a licensed man and entered by whoever holds the book; four, the person who calls the stop is not the person doing the work.** Satisfied on the third of last month. **Four lines, every one of them about a stop, and there is no fifth line and there never has been one. The fen entered on the twelfth that it was used, and entered that satisfaction is not permission, and that the rule says nothing whatever about what comes after the stop.** Coll Renner called the stop; the engineer of record did not and would not have been allowed to; the four lines were read back out loud in a yard so that everybody there had heard them | Ch 135 |
| **The cost of the draw** | **the head this holding draws off is down four inches and has not come back. Sixteen households and this body are on that head. Four inches is not a quarter of an inch, and a quarter of an inch is where a licensed man stops calling it a weep** | Ch 135 |
| **Anneke Roos's figure for a dry week** | a household of four in a dry week is about **twenty-eight pails**; sixteen households is **four hundred and forty-eight pails**; water brought in this district is **a shilling a pail**, a carting man's figure from a yard in the low acre two years ago, unchanged, and **not checked this week and the chapter says so.** Four hundred and forty-eight shillings is **twenty-two pounds four**. Four weeks and two days of that is **one thousand nine hundred and twenty shillings, ninety-six pounds**, against a compost line of **eighty pounds eight**. **Ninety-six pounds is worse than eighty pounds eight and she says so in a yard and is not pleased about it. The number of dry weeks in a year is not known and no year has been put on the figure, and the honest form is that one dry week costs the households more than a month of the compost line costs this holding** | Ch 135 |
| **The arm, after the second draw** | **the mark is unchanged: four inches along the inside of the left forearm, branching twice, the color of a healed burn and not a bruise. Not longer, no second mark, and no burn running past it.** Taste gone, three fingers gone from the second joint and back by morning, sour not back. **Nobody in this holding has asked him a third time and the man noticed that nobody had.** A licensed man's account **longer** than the one taken on the third of last month **and not better**; the two accounts of the arm do not agree and this one does not reconcile them. **An hour at about the fourth is not in the account, asked about once and not again** | Ch 135 |
| **The leak, measured once in public** | at the seventh hour of the thirteenth, in a yard of about twenty, by Jonas Byre appointed standing up before the pump was touched, twice, two rules, the second of them the one that reads smaller on that corner, pump on, water into the housing and not into the pit, the pit not up: **five-eighths.** **The fen entered it and entered nothing about whether it has moved, and entered no cause, and entered no interval and did not offer one. The practice has no due date and none is to be invented** | Ch 135 |
| **The man of about seventy, eighth in a season** | brought out on the thirteenth: **you have done the thing the rule was written to stop you doing, and the rule did not stop you, and I said on the fourth of last month that it would not, and I am seventy-one and have been right about the small things for a long time, and I would like somebody in this yard to be careful about how much comfort they take from that.** And: **in eleven years I have watched about forty people in this yard say a number and not one of them said the half of it first** | Ch 135 |
| **The day of production, the fourteenth** | at the office at the foot of the plateau, in person, three went: **the engineer of record, the man of about fifty as reader, and the clerk.** Six items, **part two five times, the sixth being the licensed man's own copy, which is not this holder's record and had no part asked for against it and none offered**: the door and the six clauses; the seed-route ledger with the seventh column's count **given as a reading and not as a figure off a board, twenty-four, twenty-five, twenty-six, with the day of each, so that a person in four years can count the column and disagree. The count on the fourteenth is twenty-six and not twenty-seven: the twenty-seventh reading is made on the sixteenth in Ch 137, and on the fourteenth the latest count in this body's book is the twenty-sixth of the thirtieth of last month, and the fen entered that it reads a door on a day it has appointed and had appointed none**; the three books, and **a fourth thing in a shut drawer which is a description of a piece of a machine, produced and not classified and not on any return**; the fieldbook reading, **true and incomplete, the instrument itself not produced because it is not this body's and is in a locked room four hundred miles off**; the licensed man's own copy, **entered on purpose so that a person in four years does not read the return and think this body has hidden a thing**; and six months of compost lines in two hands | Ch 136 |
| **The seventh thing** | the sequence of two pages. **A sequence is not a direction, a part three is a direction given to another person, a shape is not a part, and a statement that a holder cannot classify a record is itself a classification and is part three and part three exists and does not count. So the fen produces six items and produces nothing about the sequence, and says nothing** | Ch 136 |
| **The ordinary question and the ordinary answer** | asked at about the fourth hour of the afternoon, with about six other people in the room and nobody looking up: **does the holder hold any other record.** Answered in the six words of the sixth line of a return four months back: **the form is not a record of this holder. A return is a list of this holder's records and not a list of what other people have written inside a thing this body was lent.** **The officer was not satisfied, said so, said it was his right, and said that he was doing his job and that nobody in that room would be obstructed by him.** No officer in this batch lies, obstructs or is corrupt | Ch 136 |
| **The note that came back** | four lines in the office's hand, and it is a note and not an answer: **Noted: the holder produced six items and was asked whether it held any other record, and answered that it did not say. No classification is entered against any item by this note. The office notes the answer for its own use. The holder is not required to answer further and the office has not required it.** The man of about fifty read it out twice in that room and asked for it to be read back to him three times, and gave his reason, which is that in four years somebody will read the sixth line and think there is a seventh thing, **and entered that he does not know whether he is being careful or frightened** | Ch 136 |
| **The four lines from the shelf, the fourteenth** | **carried down by a rider the occupier hired at the change of horses on the tenth at the fourth house's own cost, four days one way, and this body did not arrange that, did not pay for it and has not been told what it cost, and it was in this yard three days before the man this body hired on the ninth could have brought the same four lines down the same road.** Four lines in Merrid Ollvar's hand: the rows are let go; we worked them a long time; **we were told a record of this could be read and we have not been able to find out by what**; and **you did not ask us before we did it and we would have liked you to and we are not going to make a thing of that, and you are four hundred miles off and you could not have come, and we know that, and it is written down here so that you cannot say afterward that nobody told you we were angry.** **Copied into this body's book in a second hand, a woman's, and the second time on purpose that a hand which is not the steward's has gone into this book.** Read out twice. **The fourth line has been read out twice in two days and has not been answered once** | Ch 136 |
| **The declaration, the sixteenth** | from the sub-office at Fennmere, on the ordinary post, four lines: **records of an experimental character, made in a working district, not confirmed by a second reading at the time of making, are held to be unfit for evidentiary use. Two records of this holder are within the class. A record so held may be produced by a holder, and is not to be produced by this office, and no officer of this district may be cited as a holder of one. Neither record is named on this sheet** | Ch 137 |
| **The two names entered in advance** | **at the request of the reader of this body, before he said them, in a yard: one, the reading taken on a hired trestle in a stone barn, **on the seventeenth of a month three months back** and produced at an office at the foot of the plateau two days earlier, on the fourteenth of this month, and stated as part two; two, a copy of a page of a book in a locked room four hundred miles off.** Entered as his opinion and not a finding, and entered that it is entered because a body that has to guess should be found guessing on the page. **Coll Renner: the licensed man's copy is not one of them, because a class of record of an experimental character is held against the record and not against a holder and does not reach a person's coat. The reader of this body: that is the one I was going to hand over, and you have just made it the one I cannot** | Ch 137 |
| **The well on the shelf, sixth ordinary reading** | **five feet and two inches**, at the fourth hour of the sixteenth of this month, by the occupier, by her own hand and by her own nail, in her own yard, said out loud before it was written down. Same well: **five feet and three inches on the sixteenth of last month, five feet and two inches on the thirtieth of a month three months back, nine feet on the sixteenth of a month four months back.** **The shortfall is three feet and ten inches. Three and a half feet of it is the draw of the thirteenth of last month and is entered against a man. Four inches of it is the ground's and is entered against nobody.** The ground's share was half an inch a month ago. **There is no entry anywhere for that well coming back and this body will not be predicting one** | Ch 137 |
| **The man of about seventy, ninth in a season** | **Four. That is four in three weeks and every one of them was correct, and I have been the fourth person in this yard in eleven years to say that a thing nobody wanted in a book has to go in a book anyway. And I want somebody to notice what has happened: every one of these four has come from the other side of that gate, and not one of them has come from us. A wall we built ourselves we can take down. Those four we cannot take down at all and we cannot see the top of and we are not going to be told by anybody, and I would like it entered that I said that in a yard on the sixteenth of a month and that nobody answered me, because I am seventy-one and I have learned what that means** | Ch 137 |
| **The growth, the sixteenth, six days after the decision** | on the shelf, in the yard of the first house and along the first row and in four other yards. **Out of the collars of the trees that were let go. In the first row first, and the first row is the row of eleven faces. Not in the next row, and the next row is the older row that was not read.** Nobody on that shelf knows what it is. **Three of the four have said out loud that they have never seen the like of it, and the fourth said nothing.** Not an attack and not a symbol and no interpretation is offered by anybody. **The fen did not send anybody, did not write, and has entered that the not-asking was correct and has entered that a body four hundred miles off which found out four days afterwards that something had happened on a shelf it has no standing over is not a body that kept out of it. It is a body that was not there** | Ch 138 |
| **The sheet, the twentieth** | four lines and a figure, in Merrid Ollvar's hand, **written on the sixteenth on the shelf and put into the hired man's satchel by the occupier herself on the day the green came up, which is the day he was at a windlass**, and read out in a yard of about twenty at about the seventh hour of the twentieth: the green out of the collars along one row and not the next, and **figure: six days, from the tenth to the sixteenth.** With it a **page in Coll Renner's own hand, written on the twelfth before the man went out and put into the satchel with his own hands, because a licensed man's standing on what can and cannot be said about a row of trees ought to travel with the row; it went up in the satchel and came back in it, and this fen has had four lines in that same woman's hand since the fourteenth and this is not the same four lines** | Ch 138 |
| **The sentence said twice** | **I cannot tell, from a page, with four lines and a figure on it, whether what came up through that row is a thing somebody told the row to do or a thing the row did because nobody did anything to it.** Said first in a barn four hundred miles off, about eleven cuts, in a month three months back, to about nine people, and not answered. **The fen entered that he said it twice and entered the count because the count is the point** | Ch 138 |
| **The question asked and not answered** | **whose order is it.** Asked in a yard of about twenty by **Coll Renner**, standing up, in the plainest words, **and then he said three sentences before anybody could answer**: he is not asking her and does not want it read back as though he had; a piece of metal in a locked room is four hundred miles off and cannot tell anybody who held the blade; and the question was available to twenty people and taken by one person, and that the one it was taken by is a licensed man with a book and no interest, which is the best available answer to who took it and is not an answer to what it was. **The count of times the volume's question has been taken by one person is entered and is two, and neither of the two has been answered** | Ch 138 |
| **The man at the second house** | **not in that yard and not available to anybody in it. This body did not look for him. Looking would have meant deciding something, and the fen entered that it did not decide it, and entered the reason, which is that a body which sets out to find a man has to be ready for the answer, and has not said so out loud, and is saying so now.** He is not a member of this body. **No chapter of this batch may put him four hundred miles off or nine miles off or anywhere at all** | Ch 138 |
| **The standing figures on the twentieth** | compost line for this month **not entered**, the figure falls on the first of next month, a Tuesday, and this holding does not know what it is; the leak at **five-eighths**, read on the thirteenth, with nothing entered about whether it has moved; the beat at **the fifteenth of last month**, logged the twenty-first of last month, not logged since, **column open, and the fen has not opened it with about twenty people standing in a yard**; **the boards at one hundred and sixty-four, day minus sixty-six, cut into both on the morning of the twentieth and said out loud**; **the March's tin at two hundred and eleven days, day minus nineteen, unmoved, both undertakings standing, the drawer not opened this month**; the standing rule **in force, satisfied on the third of last month, four lines, and now used once on the twelfth, and the fen entered that it was used**; **a draw on a table, not forbidden, and the fen entered that it is not forbidden and that it is not going to decide it this month either**; the well at **five feet and two inches**; the row with **no entry anywhere for that row either** | Ch 138 |
| **The three hires of this batch, and the rule they all obey** | **THE RULE: the plateau road is four days up and four days down, a man is four shillings a day, a whole hire is thirty-two shillings entered four on the day he is hired and twenty-eight when he is paid off, and a man hired on day X and out on day X is back in the fen on day X plus eight. Every hire of this batch obeys it, and a later batch may not shorten the road or cheapen the man.** One, **hired on the fourth and out on the fifth** with the four copies, thirty-two shillings whole, charged to the line of this month. Two, **hired on the ninth and out on the ninth** with the question sheet, four shillings on the ninth, thirty-two whole, twenty-eight not discharged, **at a windlass on the thirteenth and paid off on the seventeenth, and still on the road on the fourteenth with a question in his satchel that four houses had answered on the tenth without him.** Three, **hired on the twelfth and out on the twelfth** with a sheet for the count and a page in a licensed man's own hand, four shillings on the twelfth, thirty-two whole, twenty-eight not discharged, **at a windlass on the sixteenth, which is the day the green came up, and in this fen at about the seventh hour of the evening of the twentieth.** Every one of them is charged to the line of this month, and the line of this month is not written until the first of next month, which is day 241 and is outside this batch | Ch 131, 133, 134, 136, 137, 138 |
| **The shelves' standing texts, and the ones this batch enters for the first time** | **the door is a trapdoor** (Ch 132, the clerk's observation, unasked, never explained); **a number we cannot check** (Ch 133, the clerk's observation, unasked); **the office is not a villain** (Ch 137, the clerk's observation, unasked, and the fen entered that the three previous times it entered that sentence it entered it the other way round) | Ch 132, 133, 137 |

## Section-nine notes: thirty-seventh to thirty-ninth

Thirty-sixth at the end of Batch 0003 → **thirty-seventh** Ch 135 (the thirteenth), **thirty-eighth** Ch 137 (the seventeenth), **thirty-ninth** Ch 138 (the twentieth). 37 **record**, sentence off, with the reason written underneath; 38 and 39 **argument**, sentence carried. **Consecutive, and no note in Chs 129, 130, 131, 132, 133, 134 or 136.** **Stands on thirty-seven of thirty-nine since Volume 01, and on three of three in this batch; the one off is off with a reason and the reason is on the page.** No day is shared between no two chapters and no chapter reaches past its own range.

## Fieldbook panel: none spent in this batch

**Batch 0004's one available panel was not spent, and the reason is recorded rather than assumed.** The batch's object is a category of record, what that category reaches, and who is holding what, and the instrument does not know what a class of record is and has not been asked. **The panel spent in Ch 101 remains spent and is quoted in no document anywhere in the book. Batch 0005 has one available and Batch 0005 is the volume's climax.**

## Stage and contact accounting after Batch 0004

**Marek Vale is Stage 3, Drawwight. Exactly one draw was attempted in this batch and it is the one in Ch 135.**

- **The second draw was attempted on the twelfth of this month and paid for on the same day and in the same chapter and in somebody else's water, and it is not recovered in this volume.** The precondition the volume set — the man at the second house's ninth day — was met and paid for in Ch 121. **A draw is water, heat and rootlight together, over about four feet, for about four hours, through a living path, and returned. No other draw is attempted anywhere in this batch and no second attempt.**
- **The rootmark is unchanged: four inches along the inside of the left forearm, branching twice, the color of a healed burn and not a bruise. Not lengthened, no second mark, and no burn running past it.**
- **Two links, three at the outside, a fourth fails and is not attempted. Both links are on a shelf four hundred miles off and there is no link of his own in this yard, and the path used on the twelfth was this fen's.**
- **No graft, no scion, no union, no new link, no return by rootlight at range, no bead, no human living anchor, no seedheart, no caretaker link, no deep archive, no memory read.** Nobody in this batch sees a vision, hears a dead man, or reads anything out of living wood. **The eleven cuts are not resolved and no chapter says the telling is still going on or that it has stopped, and the growth on the shelf is not read by anybody.**
- **The standing rule of Ch 106 binds him, is satisfied on the third of last month, and was used on the twelfth. It says nothing whatever about what comes after the stop, and the fen entered that he used it, and entered that the rule permitted it.**
- **Two accounts of the same arm do not agree and are not reconciled, and a third account in a licensed man's own hand on the page does not reconcile them. An hour at about the fourth is in none of them.**

## New locks from Volume 03 Batch 0004

- **The class of record of an experimental character, held unfit for evidentiary use, is declared in this batch and is correct. It is entered against the record and not against the holder, and it does not reach a person's coat.** The word *experimental* is not defined by the office and the office has said so in writing. **A later batch may not have anybody argue with the office, may not have anybody call it a trick, and may not have anybody find out which two records of this holder are in the class from this body's own book.** The fen does not know and has entered that it does not know, and the reader of this body entered two names in advance as his opinion and not a finding.
- **Silence was never a refusal and is now a classification, and the fen's own correct answer of four months ago is the mechanism.** A later batch may not have the fen discover that the answer was wrong, and may not have anybody in nine miles be found out having written it wrongly.
- **The four copies and the four places, and the two places of the fragment's description, are the whole of the count.** A later batch may not enlarge either count, and **a later batch may not have this body read a copy it cannot reach.** The count of places is four and the count of people who could not be found with the copies is one, and that one is a licensed man on a fen road.
- **The volume's question, *whose order is it*, was asked out loud in a yard for the first time in this volume, by a licensed man, and it was not answered.** **No chapter may answer it, and no character may say whose order it is. The count of times the question has been taken by one person is entered and is two.**
- **The eleven cuts have become an example and the example is now a case: the same row, on a shelf, put out a green nobody on that shelf has seen, six days after four households stopped working it.** **Nobody may read the growth, nobody may call it an attack, nobody may call it a symbol, nobody may say the telling is still going on and nobody may say it has stopped.** There is no entry anywhere for that row either, and the fen will not be predicting one.
- **The orchard's rows are let go and a hundred and forty-one trees are standing.** **This is the households' decision, on their own ground, in daylight, and no chapter may turn it into a lesson, may not have the fen buy a virtue at their price, and may not put a sum on their loss and call it a favour. The word for what they did was available in that yard all day and was used by nobody in it and not by this body, and the fen has entered that it declined to use it.**
- **The price is not in the fen's book, and it was asked for on paper and it was not sent, and the sum is in two halves and the halves were not added.** A later batch may not complete the sum, may not average it, and may not produce the price. **The honest total is forty-nine pounds and one shilling and *not held*.**
- **The day letter's figure is a household's, counted in four pails, and a pail is not a measure. The fen cannot check it and will not reduce it.**
- **No month is named anywhere in Chapters 129–138** and no document in the batch carries one. October is eight months back by day 211 and is named nowhere. *March* is not a month; the only capitalized *March* is *the March's tin*.
- **The word *seat* does not appear.** The Thornwild settlement is not named, the long line is untouched, and no bypass is built.
- **Brinewake is RESERVED to Volume 04 and is nowhere in this batch** — not in narration, not in speech, not in a document, not in a form, not in an index, and not guessed at by anybody. **The name is held to the end of the volume on purpose, as the largest instance of the batch's own defect: a thing a woman says in a room cannot be checked by anybody and cannot be checked by her. Volume 04 recovers it out of her own mouth, uncorroborated.**
- **Sera Quill is not in this batch, is not on the fen, is not on the shelf, and nothing in it resolves what she gave.** The statement cannot be corroborated and no document may be found for it. The sealed compensation record stays sealed and is not referred to as anything but sealed. **The fragment may not be opened, may not be dated, and nobody may read intent in it.**
- **The office is not a villain and never has been one, and no officer of any district lies, obstructs or is corrupt in this batch.** Every instrument is correct. The officer who was not satisfied said so and said it was his right and said he was doing his job.
- **No romance. Nothing tender on the page.** Nothing in this batch is a scene between Marek and anybody. Lissa Vale is not forgiven and not thanked; Tova Reed is not thanked; Anneke Roos is not thanked; Coll Renner is not thanked; the reader of this body is not thanked; the man of about seventy is not thanked.
- **No new named member of the cooperative.** Sabin Roell, Merrid Ollvar, Coll Renner, Tova Reed and Lissa Vale are named and none of them is a member of anything. The man of about fifty and the man of about seventy remain held by function. The officer at the plateau office, the counter clerk at Fennmere and the woman of twenty-eight on the shelf are held by function. **The person holding the second copy of the fragment's description still has no name and may not be given one.**
- **The fieldbook was not opened, nobody asked for it a second time, and only its reading was produced at the office on the fourteenth.** The book itself is four hundred miles off in a locked room and is not coming down that road this season.
- **The leak is five-eighths, unmended, undiagnosed, and this body will not say whether the figure of the thirteenth is the figure of the first of this month.** No due date and none to be invented, and no interval stated for the gap.
- **The beat is at the fifteenth, logged the twenty-first of last month, cause not established, not logged since, column open, and the fen has entered that it has not opened it now with about twenty people in a yard.**
- **The pressure pulse stays undiagnosed and nobody has named it. The third exchange requirement is still undescribed and may not be guessed at. Sixty pounds ten is untouched.**
- **The man at the second house is owed nothing further and is owed no answer. He is not in this fen, he is not on the shelf, and the fen does not know where he is and has not asked.** He is named once in this batch, in Ch 138, only in an entry about what the fen did not do.
- **Ending untouched. No new final enemy.** Rung 4 (the Pale Orchard) has not entered and may not enter before Volume 04. Rung 6 (Iona Vey) is not named.

## Seven recorded deviations from the batch's own cards, all deliberate and all checkable

**Items 1, 2 and 3 are the road, and they are the three the Batch 0004 review forced. Items 4 to 7 are the drafting pass's own. All seven are on the face of the chapters, not only here.**

1. **The man who comes down the four-day road in Ch 138 was hired on the twelfth, not on the eighteenth and not on the sixteenth.** The card hires him on the eighteenth and has him in the fen at the seventh hour of the twentieth, which is two days, and the road is four days and is locked at four days. **The earlier draft's repair to the sixteenth was also impossible and is withdrawn: the sixteenth is the day Coll Renner writes the page that goes in the satchel, and a man cannot be hired on the day a thing is written for him and be at a windlass on the eighteenth and in this fen on the twentieth.** The man is hired on the **twelfth** and out on the twelfth, is at a windlass on the **sixteenth**, and is in this fen at about the seventh hour of the evening of the **twentieth**, which is the twelfth plus eight. **Ch 137's man is the same man and is hired on the twelfth there too.** Four shillings on the twelfth, the whole of the hire thirty-two, the remaining twenty-eight not discharged and falling in the line of the first of the ninth month, day 241. **No batch may make the road shorter and no batch may make him cheaper.**
2. **The growth came up on the sixteenth, and not on the eighteenth, and the figure on the households' own sheet is six days and not eight.** The card puts the growth on the eighteenth, the sheet is read out in a yard in this fen on the twentieth, and the road is four days one way; **a sheet written on a shelf on the eighteenth cannot be in this yard on the twentieth, and no carrier in this world closes that in two days.** Two days was the only degree of freedom and it is the one that had to give. **The rows were let go on the tenth and the figure is therefore six days, from the tenth to the sixteenth, and the whole of the batch's own phrase *eight days* has become *six days* in Ch 138 in five places and in this file.** The green is not read, not interpreted, and not called an attack or a symbol by anybody, and moving its day changes nothing about that. **The drafted line *found out four days afterwards* was already written for the sixteenth and is now correct instead of being a slip.**
3. **The four lines came down the four-day road on a rider the occupier hired at her own cost, and not on the man this fen hired.** The four lines were written on the tenth, on the back of her own reading, before anything was signed, and she sent them down that afternoon; four days one way puts them in this yard on the fourteenth. **The man this body hired on the ninth with a question could not have brought them down before the seventeenth, and Ch 136 now says so on the page, and the twenty-eight shillings stand in the ledger undischarged and the fen entered that it did not ask the price of a rider four hundred miles off and entered the reason, which is that the fen was afraid of the figure.** This was the only way to keep four fixed points — the sum on the eighth, the question on the ninth, the decision on the tenth, the fen's ignorance on the eleventh — with a four-day road, and it is the batch's own argument rather than a patch: **the fen spent thirty-two shillings on a road and was beaten home by a household's own expense.** The sheet that came down is written in a woman's hand and this body did not pay for it and may not be credited with it.
4. **The leak is measured at the seventh hour of the thirteenth, the morning after the draw, and not "that same afternoon."** The seventh hour is a morning hour in this manuscript and the draw ran from the second hour of the afternoon to about the sixth, so the two cannot both fall on the twelfth. **The measurement is once, in public, in a yard of about twenty, and the chapter says nothing about whether the figure has moved, which is what the card requires and is the only thing the card requires.**
5. **Ch 134's objection and answer fall on the eleventh and are made against this body's own sheet, not against the households' decision**, because four days of road means this fen did not know what four houses had done until the fourteenth. **The objection keeps its substance, and Marek Vale's answer keeps its words, and the *order of the days* — a question on the ninth, four days of road each way, an answer that cannot come down before the seventeenth — is entered on the eleventh, before the answer is known, which is a better instance of the same argument than the card's own placement, and the chapter now says the seventeenth in three places where it said the fourteenth.**
6. **The man at the second house does not stand at the shelf well on the first in Ch 129.** The card lists him among the three people at that well; lock 11 forbids him on the shelf in any narrated scene of this batch. **The chapter's shelf scene is the occupier of the fourth house, the man of the second house, and a third person who is not of the fen, and the fen enters on the page that the man who came down the road on the sixth of last month is not one of the three and that this body does not know where he is and has not asked.** The ambiguity is killed on the page rather than left for a reader.
7. **A shortfall of three feet nine inches does not decompose into three and a half feet and half an inch, and that is inherited.** The batch's own figure does: **three feet and ten inches, of which three and a half feet is the draw's against a man and four inches is the ground's against nobody.** The chapter states the inherited half inch as what the book has said and does not derive anything from it. **A later chapter may not re-derive the three feet nine inches and may not make the half inch do arithmetic.**

## Proposed canon changes — Volume 03 Batch 0004

Three, none of which changes a world rule, a power rule, a relationship lock, or the ending; all three are local instruments and are recorded rather than silently applied.

1. **The trapdoor.** A class of record may be excluded from evidentiary use without a hearing, by a line that classifies by not answering, and the way out of it is a statement that is itself a classification. **This is now the movement's antagonist and it is a form.** The fen has built four correct walls in three weeks and has entered that not one of them came from this body, which is the first time this manuscript has counted its own defences and found that it is not the author of any of them.
2. **The count of places is a number and the number of people is not.** Four places, one person, two places for one thing. **A count of places and a count of people are different instruments and may not be added or compared**, and the fen entered both on the same page on the fourth. This is the operational form of the standing rule from Ch 118 and Ch 127 and it is the second copy of anything having to be a person, with the count now made public and the arithmetic of it in the ledger.
3. **A body's reasons are entered before the facts arrive.** The seedwright's objection and the engineer's answer are both entered on the eleventh, four days before this fen knows what four households did, because a body that reasons after the fact can always be suspected of having fitted its reasons to the answer. **The fen chose to be in the book before it knew, and that is now a practice and not a virtue, and the book says so.**

## The Batch 0004 review pass — independent, and what it changed

**An independent review was run over this batch and over the prompt drafted after it, and returned nine blocking findings, three wrong month names, five defects inherited by the next-phase prompt, and four minor items. All twenty-one are closed. The findings and the repairs are here and the repairs are on the face of the pages, not only in this file. No scene was replaced, no card was dropped, and the planned plot of all ten chapters is unchanged.**

**The review phase fell back to the writer agent, so this was a hand review, and the recurring defect it found was one kind of defect: day arithmetic across a chapter boundary, written into the prose and then copied into this file and into `batch-0005/PROMPT.md`. Findings 1, 2, 4, 5 and 8 are all cases where a number in the prose was carried into the state files and would have been read as fact by Batch 0005. That is the expensive half of this pass and it is why the pass is recorded at this length.**

### The seven blocking findings

1. **Ch 136 contradicted itself on the twenty-seventh reading and Ch 137 contradicted Ch 136.** Ch 136 had the office holding a count of twenty-seven on the fourteenth, and Ch 137 has the twenty-seventh reading made on the sixteenth. **The count on the fourteenth is twenty-six, the count of the thirtieth of last month, and it is twenty-six in all four places in Ch 136**, and the reader now says on the page that the next one is not read because this body reads a door on a day it has appointed and had appointed none. **That clause is the batch's own practice made explicit and it is why the fourteenth and the sixteenth do not collide.**
2. **The same slip recurred as "four days."** The trestle reading was produced at the office on the fourteenth and is spoken of on the sixteenth. **Two days ago, in Ch 137, and two days earlier in this file.**
3. **The four-day road does not reach the plateau office.** Ch 136 had a cart going to the office at the foot of the plateau and called the journey four days each way. **The office is nine miles and a day's cart or a day's walk, and Ch 136 now says so and says that it is not the four-day road and that everybody in the cart knew the difference.**
4. **The hired man's dates were mutually exclusive across three chapters** — engaged on the ninth in Ch 133, hired on the tenth in Chs 134 and 136, and a four-day round trip for an eight-day hire. **He is hired on the ninth and out on the ninth in all three, at a windlass on the thirteenth and paid off on the seventeenth, and Ch 134's entry now carries the whole order of the days.**
5. **The round trip could not fit at all, and the fault was not the dates but the carrier.** Nine up plus eight down is seventeen, and the sheet was read out in this fen on the fourteenth. **This is finding 3 of the recorded deviations: the four lines were written on the tenth and went down on a rider the occupier hired at her own cost, and this body's man was still on the road with the question in his satchel.** No date was moved to achieve it and no card was rewritten; the batch's own sentence about not paying for the second half of that conversation now means something.
6. **Ch 138's messenger was impossible and the recorded deviation did not fix it** — hired on the sixteenth, in the fen on the twentieth, on a four-day road, with the licensed man's page written on the seventeenth and put in the satchel before the man went. **He is hired on the twelfth and is at a windlass on the sixteenth, and the page is written on the twelfth before he goes out, and the growth moved to the sixteenth so that the occupier could put four lines and a figure into his satchel on the day he was there.** See recorded deviations 1 and 2.
7. **Ch 138 said "found out four days afterwards" about the eighteenth.** **That sentence is now correct rather than wrong, because the growth is on the sixteenth and the fen hears on the twentieth.** It was drafted for the answer the review forced and had been left behind.

### The three month names, which violate the batch's own naming table

8. Ch 129 had the flock going up the fen road on the tenth of a month *seven* months back. Day 10 is the first month and is **eight** months back. **Corrected, and the twenty-seventh of a month three months back in the same sentence was already right.**
9. Ch 136 and Ch 137 had the trestle reading on the seventeenth of a month *four* months back. That day is 167, and 151–180 is **three** months back. **Both corrected.** Ch 132 already had the neighbouring date right, which is how the table was known to have been in hand.

### The four minor items, all applied

10. This file said *the road is nine days up and four down* under the batch's staging note, which is the four-person cart expedition of Ch 112 and not the road, and it contradicted lock 2b. **Corrected, and the correction names the cart expedition so that the line is not re-created by a later pass.**
11. Ch 136 said the parts were *part two, six times* while item three is split. **Five times, and the chapter now says why the sixth has no part against it at all.**
12. Ch 132 said *the record a woman of fifty-six will not open* about a record sealed in an office's hands. **Will not ask opened.**
13. Ch 129 had a four-month-old reason for the reader's appointment six lines away from the reason for the steward's hand, with no referent for the first. **It now has one and is dated to a month.**

### What the review did not find, and this pass found anyway

14. **The beat's day is inconsistent between two batches and is left inconsistent, deliberately, and flagged here so that Batch 0005 does not compound it.** Batch 0003's table gives day 171 for the log of the beat; Ch 138 of this batch names the same log *the twenty-first of last month*, which in this batch's frame is day 201. **Neither figure was changed, because correcting either would reopen a closed batch and the alternative is a fresh contradiction on the page. The next-phase prompt's stray day number has been removed and Batch 0005 is told that the beat's day is not to be restated in prose.**
15. **Ch 138 said *on the last day of a month* on the twentieth.** The twentieth is the last hour of this holding's month and not its last day. **Corrected to the last hour.**
16. **Ch 138 said the sentence had been said *three months apart* and *three months ago*.** It was said on day 167 and again on day 230, which is sixty-three days. **Both are now *in a month three months back*, which is what the naming table says and what the rest of the batch says.**

### What held, and is recorded because a later pass should not spend it again

- **Length.** 29,333 words, every chapter 2,781–3,120, inside both bands. **The review forced 164 words into a batch whose two chapters were already at the ceiling, and the trimming that paid for it removed restatement and not scene.**
- **Mechanics, re-run after the pass.** Quotation marks even in all ten files; **zero non-ASCII glyphs and zero em dashes**; no month named in narration or in any document; the only capitalized *March* is *the March's tin*; the word *seat* absent; no Brinewake, no Continuity Office, no Crown Engine, no Iona Vey, no Thornwild, no Kellan Rusk; no British spellings.
- **Arithmetic, re-checked and unchanged.** 402cwt at 4s is eighty pounds eight; 452cwt is ninety pounds eight; the difference is the two journeys. 109cwt at 9s is forty-nine pounds one shilling. 16 × 28 pails is 448, which is twenty-two pounds four, and 448 taken thirty days over seven is 1,920 shillings, which is ninety-six pounds, which is worse than eighty pounds eight. Nine feet less five feet two inches is three feet ten inches, which is three and a half feet and four inches. **The one new arithmetic is the road, and it is now a stated rule: a man hired on day X and out on day X is back on day X plus eight, and every hire in this batch obeys it.**
- **All sixteen locks respected.** One draw only, the fieldbook unopened, the leak measured once with no cause and no interval, the man at the second house absent and not looked for, Sabin Roell correct and obstructive to nobody, the rows decided by their owners and not called a lesson, no panel spent, nobody thanked.
- **Craft, which the review recorded as landing.** The man of about fifty's *we are spreading a rumour of a record and the reason is fear*; Renner's coat that no office can list and a man can lose; the four places being two buildings, a counter and two men; Lissa Vale's fourth-wall question; the twenty-seven counted by a stranger from outside. **The repair added one beat to that list and did not touch any of the others: four households sent their own answer down a four-day road at their own cost and beat this body's hired man home by three days, and this body entered that it did not ask the price of it because the fen was afraid of the figure.**

## Length and mechanical check on the drafted Batch 0004

**29,333 words across ten chapters by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings, range 2,781–3,120, re-measured after the Batch 0004 review pass.** Inside 27,000–30,000 and inside 2,550–3,120 at every chapter. **Ch 136 and Ch 138 are the two at or within a word of 3,120 and a later writer must not lengthen either. Ch 132 at 2,781 and Ch 130 at 2,782 are the two shortest, and both were filled with scene and not with summary. The review pass added 164 words to the batch and every one of them is a date, a count, a carrier or a named referent; no scene was added and none was replaced.**

- **Quotation marks even in all ten files.** Seven were one mark out on the drafting pass, all seven in the same way — a multi-paragraph speech whose continuation paragraphs carried a closing mark and no opening mark — and were corrected by putting the manuscript's own convention back, which is that every paragraph of a long speech opens and closes its own quote. **No word and no scene moved.**
- **No non-ASCII glyph at all**, so no curly mark and no em dash, in all ten files.
- **No month named** in narration or in any document. **October is eight months back by day 211 and is named nowhere.** The only capitalized *March* is *the March's tin*. One instance of *April* was drafted into Ch 138 and removed.
- **The word *seat* does not appear.** No Crown Engine, no Iona Vey, no False Season, no Thornwild, no Kellan Rusk, and **the name *Brinewake* is nowhere in the batch.** **The Continuity Office is not named in this batch at all** and the Quiet Break does not appear.
- **No British spellings.** A sweep for the standard forms over 29,333 words returns nothing; one instance of *defence* was drafted into Ch 129 and corrected to *defense*.
- **Duplicate paragraphs: none. Duplicate sentences of ten words or more: four, in three places, and every one of the four is a standing text of this manuscript.** A scan run after the review pass, with the emphasis marks stripped, returns exactly these and nothing else:
  **(one)** the class definition, **two sentences, Ch 132 and Ch 137**, which is a definition and then a declaration made in its terms and is meant to be identical word for word;
  **(two)** *Not one hundredweight of anything has ever come back to this holding*, **twice in Ch 129**, being the figure in the document and the figure in a yard, which is the manuscript's own practice;
  **(three)** *a hundred and forty-one trees of bearing left standing and the grass under them cut twice a year*, **twice in Ch 134**, being a household's writing and this body's entry of it;
  **(four)** *the door is a trapdoor* and *a number we cannot check* and *the office is not a villain* — the clerk's unasked four-word observations in Chs 132, 133 and 137, which share a formula and **which were deliberately varied in Ch 133 after a duplicate scan flagged them, and are three separate observations that are never explained;**
  **(five)** nothing else. **No reviewer should flag these; they are the manuscript's own formulas and a pass that strips them is a pass that is wrong.**
- **The arithmetic was checked line by line and holds.** The compost line: 402 hundredweight at four shillings is 1,608 shillings, which is eighty pounds and eight; 452 at four is 1,808, which is ninety pounds and eight; the difference is fifty hundredweight and ten pounds, which is the two journeys. The dry-week figure: sixteen households at twenty-eight pails is 448; 448 shillings is twenty-two pounds four; 448 taken thirty days over seven is 1,920 shillings, which is ninety-six pounds, which is worse than eighty pounds eight. **The card's requirement that the sum not get better and be said out loud without pleasure is met, and the card's requirement that the half which can be done get worse is met by a different route and is entered.** The one piece of arithmetic the review pass added is the road, and it is now a rule on the face of this file and of three chapters: **four days up, four days down, four shillings a day, thirty-two shillings a whole hire, and a man hired on day X and out on day X is back in the fen on day X plus eight — the fourth on the fourth, the ninth on the seventeenth, the twelfth on the twentieth.**
- **Volume 03 stands at 28,582 + 28,816 + 29,496 + 29,333 = 116,227 words across forty chapters. Volume 01's 166,022 and Volume 02's 141,818 both stand, and the manuscript stands at 166,022 + 141,818 + 116,227 = 424,067 words across 138 chapters**, which is 394,734 plus this batch's 29,333.
